i THE following is the history of the present volume. Lambert, in preparing
O OXFORD UNIVERSITY PRESS 1969 a still [1968] unpublished corpus of Babylonian creation myths first planned
to include the earlier portion of Atra-hasis, and with this in view he re-
. copied the tablet known here (see pp. 40—1) under the symbol E, and iden-
TEERTEO TE OREZT BRETAIN | tified among the copies of the late Dr. E. W. Geersin Chicagothe fragments
J-N and K 8562. At that time Miss E. Reiner made known to him her
identification of K 6634 and K 13863. "This material with the other then
published fragment of Tablet 1 he prepared for publication. However,
meanwhile the big Old Babylonian tablets A and C, had cometo light inthe
British Museum, and Millard was invited to copy them for publication in
: á CT volume, to which Lambert would also contribute his copies. Dr. R. D.
: Barnett, Keeper of the Department of Western Asiatic Antiquitiés, British
z Museum, who initiated this plan, also suggested that the two authors
7 should co-operate on a critical edition of the epic. The CT volume, no. 46,
. appeared in 1965; the present volume marks the fruition of the second
| part of this plan. Millard made a first draft of the edition, contributing the
initial decipherment of A and C,. He also first worked on D, which F. R.
| Kraus had identified in Istanbul. The final manuscript 1s largely the work
| of Lambert, except for the Glossary, which is largely the work of Millard.
| The copies and collations on pls. 1I-XI are the work of Lambert. All the
original tablets have been erther copied or collated for this edition, except
V that B has been read from photographs with the aid of collations supplied
: by J. J. Finkelstein; x and y have been copied from photographs since the

originals cannot be located. 0
While this is a scholarly edition, the Introduction has been written with
the needs in view of those who are not cuneiform scholars. In doing their
work the authors have enjoyed the co-operation of numerous scholars.
Professor J. J. Finkelstein not only supplied collations of B, as mentioned
o e ms above, but also made known to us privately the results of his work on the
: o A epic, He first read the vital sign arhu in 1 280-1 and grasped the sense of

| (131)
E THE FLOOD STORY FROM RAS SHAMRA
: Ugaritica v. 167 =RS 22. 421 (8)
Ñ This small fragment, that was announced by J. Nougayrol in Académie
0 | des Inscriptions et Belles-Lettres, Comptes Rendus, 1960. 170, and published
by him in Ugaritica Y, represents the only version of the Babylonian flood
story found outside Mesopotamia so far. Unlike Atra-hastís, it covered
only the flood itself, not the creation of man and Enlil's attempts to
y diminish their numbers. It was written on a single tablet, of which the
beginning and end only survive, and it dates from about the fourteenth
- century B.C.
Atra-hasis himself begins to speak in obverse 6, and it sounds as though
] he is going to tell the story, as happens in Gilgamej x1, where the immortal
? | flood hero explains to Gilgames how he escaped death. The first five lines
] contain no similar explanation for use of the first person here. The
Ñ orthography and grammar of the tablet mark it out as having been written
in the West, but what little of the text remains suggests a good Babylonian
work of literature, not a Syrian composition.

After whom others ruled, and Sisithros, to whom Kronos revealed that
there would be a deluge on the fifteenth day of Daisios, and ordered him
to conceal in Sippar, the city of the Sun(-god), every available writing.
Sisithros accomplished all these things, immediately sailed to Armenia, |
and thereupon what the god had announced happened. On the third day, |
after the rain abated, he let loose birds in the attempt to ascertain if they |
would see land not covered with water. Not knowing where to alight, |
being confronted with a boundless sea, they returned to Sisithros. And
similarly with others. When he succeeded with a third group—they
returned with muddy feathers—the gods took him away from mankind. |
However, the boat in Armenia supplied the local inhabitants with wooden |
amulets as charms.

(138) l SUMERIAN FLOOD STORY 139
Since Poebel's initial publication knowledge of the standard Sumerian
literary corpus, mainly from the Nippur school, secondarily from that of
THE SUMERIAN FLOOD STOR Y Ur, has increased so much that the grammatical and lexical irregularities
by M. CIVIL | of this text, some of which were already pointed out by Poebel in PBS
1v/1. 68, are much more obvious. Most of the verbal forms, for example,
. | do not fit into the paradigms of standard Sumerian. Unless one can trace

approximately, of the complete tablet, apparently from Nippur. It was | (eg. in 202 úr with ugu renders the phrase b4'u eli), then a translation
published by Arno Poebel in 1914 (PBS v, no. 1, and pls. LXXXVI-LEXAVI cannot be offered with much assurance. Some of the more doubtful
[photos)).* Poebel himself gave a complete study of the text in PBS Iv/1. passages have been italicized in the translation. However, it would be
7—70. The text aroused considerable interest and a certain number of unwise to start building conclusions on the precise wording of still others.
studies, most of which by now offer little except historical interest, For example, the text begins with allusions to the destruction of man,
